                                           Upcycling of poly(ethylene terephthalate) waste
Cite this: Green Chem., 2025, 27,
                                           plastics to terephthalonitrile
12378                                                                      a,b
                                           Phuc T. T. Nguyen,                    Jiong Cheng,a,c Junyu Mi               a
                                                                                                                            and Ning Yan          *a,b,c

                                           Chemical upcycling of plastic waste represents an emerging approach to generate value-added chemicals
                                           and mitigate environmental impacts. Despite the signiﬁcant role of terephthalonitrile in the production of
                                           bioactive compounds and high-value materials, sustainable and eﬃcient methods for its production from
                                           plastic waste remain underexplored. In this work, we present a tandem process for the transformation of
                                           poly(ethylene terephthalate) (PET) waste into terephthalonitrile under mild conditions (≤120 °C). The
                                           process involves PET ammonolysis with ethylene glycol and ammonia, followed by liquid-phase dehydra-
                                           tion of terephthalamide using Pd catalysts via a water-transfer mechanism. The dehydration step achieves
                                           complete conversion of terephthalamide with up to 68% selectivity for the dinitrile and 32% for the
                                           mononitrile. Using electrospray ionization mass spectrometry, we identiﬁed Pd complexes, predominantly
                                           Pd dimers, as the catalytic species, regardless of the Pd precursor used. Application of the tandem system
                                           to commercial PET bottles and ﬁbers resulted in terephthalonitrile yields of 39–51 mol% based on the
Received 28th May 2025,                    PET feedstock, even in the presence of pigments and chlorine. Life cycle analysis indicated that this
Accepted 29th August 2025
                                           process reduces CO2 emissions by at least 28% compared to conventional terephthalonitrile production
DOI: 10.1039/d5gc02680f                    from p-xylene. This work introduces a promising strategy for the upcycling of PET waste into organoni-
rsc.li/greenchem                           trogen compounds with high selectivity and low environmental impact.




    Green foundation
    1. This work introduces a non-petroleum-based, mild pathway for producing terephthalonitrile and presents a selective upcycling method for polyethylene
    terephthalate (PET) waste plastics into valuable organonitrogen compounds.
    2. Utilizing Pd catalysts, the process converts up to 78% of amide groups to nitriles without generating undesired byproducts, significantly enhancing atom
    economy compared to previous gas-phase PET conversions that often resulted in cracking and coke formation. Operating at low temperatures (≤120 °C), the
    method is less energy-intensive, reduces safety concerns and lowers equipment costs. Life cycle analysis indicates at least a 28% reduction in carbon footprint
    compared to conventional terephthalonitrile production pathways.
    3. The carbon footprint of this process is expected to decrease further with forthcoming advancements in green energy and low-carbon acetonitrile
    production.

                                                                           result demonstrates the viability of the recycling process, and
                                                                           future studies can focus on the oligomerization and/or immobil-
                                                                           ization of Pd species to minimize the catalyst loss.

Tandem Thermo−electrochemical Strategy for Upcycling Waste
Poly(ethylene terephthalate) into Value-Added Aniline Derivatives
Shiji Zhong, Libo Shen, Jiakun Xue, and Yiming Mo*
       Cite This: ACS Sustainable Chem. Eng. 2025, 13, 7607−7616                     Read Online


ACCESS                        Metrics & More                             Article Recommendations               *
                                                                                                               sı Supporting Information




                                                                                                                                                                Downloaded from pubs.​acs.​org/​ascecg/​article-pdf/​13/​20/​7607/​42334187/​sc5c02544.​pdf by ZHEJIANG UNIV user on 09 September 2026
ABSTRACT: Poly(ethylene terephthalate) (PET) is one of the
most extensively used synthetic polymers. Despite its widespread
use, the recycling of PET is limited to mechanical reuse or
chemical depolymerization into low-value commodity chemicals.
Herein, we report a method for upcycling PET through a tandem
thermo−electrochemical transformation into high-value aniline
derivatives, specifically p-aminobenzoic acid (PABA) and p-
phenylenediamine (PPD). This strategy involves three cascade
catalytic steps, including thermochemical zinc acetate-catalyzed
PET glycolysis to bis(2-hydroxyethyl) terephthalate (BHET),
thermochemical BHET ammonolysis to terephthalamide (TP),
and electrochemical Hofmann (e-Hofmann) reaction of TP to
PABA and PPD. Notably, the two thermochemical steps are highly
efficient with 90.5% yield for glycolysis and 96.2% yield for ammonolysis, and only simple filtration was involved for intermediate
purification, significantly minimizing the separation cost. Instead of using hazardous chemical oxidants for the Hofmann
rearrangement, bromide-mediated e-Hofmann reaction utilized active bromine and base generated electrochemically to drive the
rearrangements of TP. The developed protocol was tested for waste PET from a wide range of sources. A preliminary
technoeconomic analysis estimated that the profit of this tandem thermo−electrochemical upcycling strategy would be $480/tonPET,
outcompeting the traditional PET recycling process.
KEYWORDS: poly(ethylene terephthalate), plastic, electrochemical synthesis, tandem catalysis, Hofmann rearrangement reaction

Life Cycle Assessment of Chemical Upcycling of Postconsumer
Polyethylene Terephthalate to Kevlar Polymer
Published as part of ACS Sustainable Chemistry & Engineering special issue “Advancing a Circular Economy”.
Jaewon Han, Preeti Nain, Richard-Joseph L. Peterson, Elanna P. Neppel, and Annick Anctil*
       Cite This: ACS Sustainable Chem. Eng. 2025, 13, 18924−18937                    Read Online




                                                                                                                                                                  Downloaded from pubs.​acs.​org/​ascecg/​article-pdf/​13/​44/​18924/​42350301/​sc5c04073.​pdf by ZHEJIANG UNIV user on 09 September 2026
ACCESS                        Metrics & More                             Article Recommendations                 *
                                                                                                                 sı Supporting Information


ABSTRACT: Due to its low cost and chemical stability,
poly(ethylene terephthalate) (PET) is widely used in single-use
items, such as plastic bottles and packaging materials. The high
demand has resulted in a significant amount of discarded PET
waste, underscoring the need for effective recycling strategies. One
promising approach is PET chemical upcycling, but it requires
evaluation of the environmental impact of the entire process, from
waste collection to polymer synthesis. This study, for the first time,
uses life cycle assessment (LCA) to assess the environmental
impact of chemical upcycling of postconsumer PET to Kevlar
polymer. The study models the life cycle inventory of chemical
upcycling via PET ammonolysis and hydrolysis, developing
scenarios based on the environmental hotspots of each chemical process for future optimization. The results show that upcycled
Kevlar (15.22 kg CO2 eq/kg Kevlar) has lower impacts than virgin Kevlar polymer production, with a 15% reduction in global
warming potential (GWP), 30% in cumulative energy demand (CED), 1% in ecotoxicity, and 40% in fossil fuel depletion. Hotspot
analysis identifies monomer preparation as the most impactful process, particularly due to the use of solvents such as chloroform and
diethyl ether. Chloroform, used in the p-phenylenediamine (PPD) preparation process, is one of the largest contributors, accounting
for approximately 20.8% of GWP and 12.1% of ecotoxicity. The best-case scenario, which replaces potassium hydroxide with sodium
hydroxide and reduces solvent consumption for purifying and extracting monomers, results in a 19.9% decrease in GWP and a 21.1%
reduction in costs compared to those of the baseline. These results highlight that upcycled Kevlar has the potential to reduce the
environmental burden of Kevlar production by utilizing PET waste. However, achieving adoption on an industrial scale requires
targeted process optimization, addressing process hotspots, and replacing conventional solvents with greener alternatives.
KEYWORDS: plastic recycling, chemical upcycling, polyethylene terephthalate, kevlar, life cycle assessment, green chemistry

                                                                            material quality in recycling methods. Mechanical recycling
                                                                            remains the more economical option but is limited to
                                                                            producing lower-grade products. Chemical recycling, while
                                                                            enabling the conversion to high-value polymers, involves
                                                                            higher costs due to complex chemical reactions and expensive
                                                                            reagents.

Figure 5. Life cycle cost of upcycled Kevlar and virgin Kevlar.

7.03 USD/kg compared with 9.23 USD/kg for virgin Kevlar.
The initial mechanical recycling of the plastic waste incurs a
minimum cost of 0.60 USD per kg of Kevlar, reflecting its
simplicity compared to the complex chemical processes. The
cost analysis of PET depolymerization via ammonolysis and
hydrolysis shows that the subsequent conversion to tereph-
thalamide and terephthalic acid contributes marginally only to
the overall costs, with $0.25/kg and $0.41/kg for Kevlar,
respectively. A detailed cost analysis of each intermediate
product and Kevlar production is provided in Tables S7 and
S8. The estimated cost from depolymerization to the
terephthalic acid process is estimated to be $1.18/kg. Previous
studies report the minimum selling price of TPA acid from
PET to be approximately $1.45/kg, excluding 25% of capital
expenditures,72 $1.05/kg via methanolysis, and $0.96/kg via
glycolysis, while the traditional virgin market price of TPA acid
is $1.14/kg. The results suggest that depolymerization is less
expensive than fossil-derived acid, even without considering
profits and market conditions.
   However, preparing PPD and TCL increases the cost, with
additional expenses of $1.41 and $1.75 per Kevlar, respectively.
TCL is often preferred for converting carboxylic acids to acid
chlorides due to its low cost, making it the most economical
method for acid chloride preparation.73 Despite this, according
to Pfizer’s amidation guide, GSK’s amidation guide, and
PubChem, the acid chloride does not meet greenness criteria,
being highly corrosive and environmentally toxic.74,75 The
excessive use of thionyl chloride and diethyl ether primarily
drives the high cost of TCL preparation for chemical
upcycling. In commercial polymer production, however, the                   Figure 6. (a) GWP and cost for intermediate products from Kevlar

Upcycling PET waste into high value-added p-xylylenediamine (PXDA): A
route to diversify PET-derived chemicals
Songlei Mo a,b, Xiaohui Liu a,b, Yanqin Wang a,b,*
a
  Shanghai Key Laboratory of Functional Materials Chemistry, Research Institute of Industrial Catalysis, School of Chemistry and Molecular Engineering, East China
University of Science and Technology, Shanghai, 200237, PR China
b
  State Key Laboratory of Green Chemical Engineering and Industrial Catalysis, Research Institute of Industrial Catalysis, School of Chemistry and Molecular Engineering,
East China University of Science and Technology, Shanghai 200237, PR China




A R T I C L E I N F O                                         A B S T R A C T

Keywords:                                                     The upcycling of polyethylene terephthalate (PET) waste into value-added chemicals is of great interest. How­
Polyethylene terephthalate                                    ever, the diversity of currently accessible PET-derived high-value products remains limited, posing a significant
Upcycling                                                     challenge to expanding their scope. Herein, we report a three-step catalytic process for converting PET waste into
PET-derived high-value products
                                                              p-xylylenediamine (PXDA)—an essential monomer for producing high-market-value polyamides, polyimides,
Terephthalonitrile
P-xylylenediamine
                                                              and nonisocyanate polyurethanes. Specifically, PET is first transformed into terephthalamide (TP) via ammo­
                                                              nolysis, and subsequently dehydrated to terephthalonitrile (TPN), the key intermediate for PXDA synthesis.
                                                              Successful separation of each intermediate ensures efficient hydrogenation of TPN to PXDA, achieving an overall
                                                              PXDA yield of 54.5 % from PET. Furthermore, the transformation of common real plastic waste and scale-up
                                                              experiments demonstrate the robustness and substantial industrial potential of this catalytic route. This study
                                                              establishes a viable and promising strategy for upcycling PET waste into high value-added product PXDA,
                                                              significantly broadening the scope of PET-derived chemicals and stimulating further exploration of other valu­
                                                              able derivatives.

of via a three-step catalytic process.                                             Writing – review & editing, Supervision, Funding acquisition,
    PXDA serves as a key component in producing polyamides, poly­                  Conceptualization.
imides, and non-isocyanate polyurethanes, exhibiting significant market
value and demand [20,21]. To further highlight the enhanced economic               Declaration of competing interest
appeal of the PET-to-PXDA process over alternative PET re/upcycling
strategies, we conducted a comparative market value analysis between                  The authors declare no competing financial interest.
PXDA and other PET depolymerization products (Fig. 4c). Obviously, it
is found that the market value of PXDA is 8638 $/ton, which is higher              Acknowledgements
than that of other PET depolymerization products, indicating the eco­
nomic competitiveness of the PET-to-PXDA process.                                     The authors thank the financially supports by the National Natural
                                                                                   Science Foundation of China (22172048), the National Key Research
4. Conclusion                                                                      and Development Program of China (2022YFA1504903) and Science
                                                                                   and Technology Commission of Shanghai Municipality (10dz2220500).
    In summary, we have established a novel and economical strategy for
upcycling PET plastic waste into high value-added p-xylylenediamine                Appendix A. Supplementary data
(PXDA) via a three-step catalytic process. This process comprises: i)
Ammonolysis of PET to terephthalamide (TP) with 85.3 % yield over a                   Supplementary data to this article can be found online at https://doi.
ZnCl₂ catalyst in THF at 220 ◦ C; ii) Dehydration of TP to tereph­                 org/10.1016/j.cej.2025.168710.
thalonitrile (TPN) with 73.1 % yield (isolated yield: 65 %) over a TiO₂
catalyst in acetonitrile at 260 ◦ C; iii) Hydrogenation of TPN to PXDA             Data availability
with 87.4 % yield over a Pd/C catalyst at 65 ◦ C. An overall PXDA yield of
54.5 % is achieved from PET. Crucially, the successful transformation of              Data will be made available on request.
real post-consumer plastic waste and scale-up experiments demonstrate
the robustness and significant industrial potential of this catalytic route.       References
This work paves the way for the selective upcycling of PET into PXDA

Upcycling Waste PET: I. Ammonolysis Kinetics of Model Dimethyl
Terephthalate and the Catalytic Effects of Ethylene Glycol
Richard-Joseph L. Peterson, Elanna P. Neppel, Lars Peereboom, P. Anh Trinh, Robert Y. Ofoli,
and John R. Dorgan*
       Cite This: ACS Sustainable Chem. Eng. 2025, 13, 4120−4131                     Read Online




                                                                                                                                                               Downloaded from pubs.​acs.​org/​ascecg/​article-pdf/​13/​10/​4120/​42342431/​sc4c10238.​pdf by ZHEJIANG UNIV user on 09 September 2026
ACCESS                        Metrics & More                             Article Recommendations               *
                                                                                                               sı Supporting Information


ABSTRACT: Chemical upcycling of waste plastics can play an
important role in developing greater circularity in the material
flows associated with the plastics industries. In this study, a
fundamental understanding of upcycling poly(ethylene tereph-
thalate) (PET) using ammonolysis is established. First, rate
constants are determined for model studies of the ammonolysis of
dimethyl terephthalate (DMT) in methanol. Ammonolysis
proceeds sequentially, and a first ester group of DMT reacts
with ammonia to produce methanol and the monoamide methyl 4-
carbamoylbenzoate (MCB). Next, MCB reacts with ammonia to
yield methanol and terephthalamide (TPD). At 100 °C, the
pseudo first order rate constants are k′1 = 0.25 ± 0.02 h−1 and k′2 =
0.11 ± 0.02 h−1. Experiments conducted at 50, 75, 100, and 125
°C yield activation energies for the first and second reactions of Ea1 = 27.9 ± 2.2 kJ/mol and Ea2 = 37.3 ± 3.3 kJ/mol. Ammonolysis
is demonstrated to be catalyzed by ethylene glycol (EG) with a first order concentration dependence. At 100 °C with EG present in
a 3:1 excess, the pseudo first order rate constants are k′3 = 6.3 ± 0.7 h−1 and k′4 = 1.7 ± 0.3 h−1, representing a 22-fold increase.
Demonstration experiments with reclaimed mixed postconsumer thermoform containers reveal that the ammonolysis of PET is self-
catalyzed by the generated EG; the upcycling reaction on polymer substrates is autocatalytic. This new detailed understanding of the
self-catalyzed chemical kinetics of ammonolysis suggests EG as the natural choice for the solvent, a topic pursued in part II of this
work.
KEYWORDS: plastic waste, poly(ethylene terephthalate), recycling, upcycling, ammonolysis, terephthalamide, reaction kinetics

   The molecular cross-section (σAB) can be thought of as the
average distance between species when they collide and is
directly proportional to the collision frequency. The decrease
in molecular weight implies a decreased molecular cross-
section which would mean a decreased collisional frequency.
So, this effect cannot explain the observed differences.
   Instead, the higher value of A2 compared to A1 may be
partially explained by the fact that the bond angle of the ester
in MCB is greater than the bond angle of the esters in DMT.

  and is most representative of how a large-scale industrial process
  should be implemented.
      Determining the start time of reactions is important in deter-
  mining reaction kinetics. However, precisely pinpointing when
  t = 0 is challenging. There are two options widely used for esti-
  mating the start time of the reaction: 1) setting time to zero when
  the reactants are mixed, or 2) setting time to zero when the reac-
  tion reaches the setpoint temperature. In contrast to these prac-
  tices, t = 0 is defined as the moment the reactor is placed in the
  thermal well and the temperature starts being recorded (which
  is only shortly after the reactants are mixed). Using this technique,
  the average temperature can be calculated from the recorded data.
  The techniques employed thus provide a high degree of accuracy
  in the reaction times and temperatures; details appear in the SI.
      Determining the mixture composition upon halting the reac-
  tion also has challenges that must be addressed. BHET and the
  intermediate, HCB, are soluble in EG. These compounds are found
  in the liquid phase, even after cooling to 5 °C. For example, after
                                                                                Figure 3. Combined NMR spectra from recovered liquid and solid samples

                                                                                sivity. While consistent with experimental observations, changes
                                                                                in diffusivity cannot fully explain the differences in collision
                                                                                factors.
                                                                                     It is notable that the present system is more complex than
                                                                                what might be expected due to hydrogen bonding effects.
                                                                                Indeed, earlier studies on the catalytic effects of EG and other
                                                                                glycols like glycerol invoke the idea that the glycols form hydro-
                                                                                gen bonding networks with the carbonyl groups of the esters. A
                                                                                full explanation of the orientation effects of hydrogen bonding in
                                                                                the system is beyond the scope of the present study.
                                                                                     Activation energies for the two reaction steps are justifiable
                                                                                using conventional molecular reasoning. Differences in activation
                                                                                energies are conventionally attributed to a combination of steric
                                                                                and electronic structure effects. For example, ester groups are
                                                                                more electron withdrawing than amide groups.[41] In BHET, two
                                                                                opposing esters within the structure exert an even pull on elec-
  Figure 5. Arrhenius plot of k1 and k2 against inverse temperature.

                                                                                ester’s stronger electron-withdrawing effect surpasses that of the

                                                                                   general, smaller molecules diffuse more rapidly than larger ones.
                                                                                   Therefore, despite the disparity, these two results are compatible.
                                                                                        Another way to analyze the diffusivity coefficient is to compare
                                                                                   it with reported values for the diffusivity of ammonia in other
                                                                                   polymers.[47,48] Comparing low-density polyethylene and polydi-
                                                                                   methylsiloxane with various fluoropolymers, Makhloufi al. reported
                                                                                   diffusivities ranging from 5.9 E-8 cm2 s1 to 4.18 E-6 cm2 s1 at
                                                                                   21 °C.[49] Accordingly, the value determined in this study (1.37 
                                                                                    0.48 E-7 cm2 s1) falls within the range of known ammonia diffu-
                                                                                   sivities in other polymers at temperatures above their glass transi-
                                                                                   tion temperatures.
                                                                                        While published literature lacks specific information on the
                                                                                   diffusion of ammonia in PET, models can provide estimates for
                                                                                   comparison. Ewender developed a function based on molecule
                                                                                   size and temperature to predict diffusion in PET.[50,51] Using this
                                                                                   model, the calculated diffusion coefficient for ammonia in PET is
                                                                                   3.18 E-8 cm2 s1, this value is approximately six times lower than
  Figure 9. Effectiveness factor versus Thiele modulus. The effectiveness fac-

Valorization of polyethylene terephthalate wastes to terephthalamide via
catalyst-free ammonolysis
Jianhao Liang a, Jinxia Fu b, Hongwei Lin a, Junyue Chen a, Shiyu Peng c, Yuyue Sun c,
Yongjun Xu a, Shimin Kang a, *
a
    Guangdong Provincial Key Laboratory of Distributed Energy Systems, Dongguan University of Technology, Dongguan, Guangdong 523808, China
b
    Hawaii Natural Energy Institute, University of Hawaii at Manoa, Honolulu, HI 96822, USA
c
    Tsinglan School, Dongguan, Guangdong 523808, China




A R T I C L E I N F O                                      A B S T R A C T

Keywords:                                                  Valorization for the recycling of polyethylene terephthalate (PET) wastes has garnered significant attention. An
Polyethylene terephthalate                                 innovative one-step approach, devoid of catalysts and solvent, was developed for the ammonolysis of PET to
Ammonolysis                                                terephthalamide (TP) at mild temperatures (40–120 ◦ C). The study extensively explored and discussed the im­
Terephthalamide
                                                           pacts of key process parameters such as temperature, reaction time, material ratio, stirring speed, and various
Respond surface methodology
                                                           PET-containing materials. The reaction conditions were further optimized via Response Surface Methodology.
                                                           The results demonstrated proposed ammonolysis technique facilitates the straightforward recovery of high TP
                                                           yield (>90 mol%) and purity (>95 %), establishing an effective avenue for TP production through PET waste
                                                           valorization.

Valorization of waste PET: Understanding the role of active ammonia in
facilitating PET depolymerization and aromatic nitrile formation
Geliang Xie a, 1, Guoqiang Zhu a, 1, Yifan Kang a, Mingxu Zhu a, Qianqian Lu b, Chao He c,
Lujiang Xu a, *, Zhen Fang a
a
    Biomass Group, College of Engineering, Nanjing Agricultural University, No. 40 Dianjiangtai Road, Nanjing, 210031, China

A R T I C L E I N F O                                        A B S T R A C T

Handling Editor: Kathleen Aviso                              Realizing the recovery and valorization of waste polyethylene terephthalate (PET) plastics into value-added
                                                             products have gained significant importance. This study successfully synthesized aromatic nitriles, specifically
Keywords:                                                    terephthalonitrile (TPN), from waste polyethylene terephthalate (PET) plastic through in situ catalytic pyrolysis
Polyethylene terephthalate                                   with urea and γ-Al2O3 catalyst. Based on distributed activation energy model, positive interaction between waste
Catalytic pyrolysis
                                                             PET and urea is responsible for improved thermal decomposition kinetics. The dosage of urea and pyrolysis
Thermodynamics
                                                             temperature significantly influenced TPN production. TPN yield reached 32.5% with ca. selectivity of 85% at
Terephthalonitrile
Density functional theory                                    550 ◦ C and equivalent urea dosage. Density functional theory calculations validated the role of active ammonia
                                                             in reducing energy barriers and thus facilitating PET depolymerization. The study highlights the feasibility of
                                                             recycling waste PET into valuable aromatic nitriles through in situ catalytic pyrolysis with urea and enhances the
                                                             understanding of the pyrolysis process.

G. Xie et al.                                                                                                              Journal of Cleaner Production 434 (2024) 140204


                                                                                         the pyrolysis reaction could lead to fewer nitriles and more char formed.
                                                                                         While, less urea also resulted in more unidentified carbon, which might
                                                                                         be the oligomer of PET decomposition that could not be detected and
                                                                                         quantified. Therefore, the dosage of urea is crucial for producing nitriles
                                                                                         via in situ catalytic pyrolysis process.
                                                                                             Table 2 also shows that the urea dosage had an important effect on
                                                                                         the production of TPN, especially when the urea dosage was less than
                                                                                         that of PET. When the urea dosage was 0.5 of PET, the carbon yield of
                                                                                         TPN was only 14.57%, while when the amount of urea reached 1 time of
                                                                                         PET, the TPN yield increased to 28.27 C%, which was a great
                                                                                         improvement. Moreover, the yields of TPN and aromatic nitriles did not
                                                                                         increase significantly with the further increase of urea dosage. Mean­
                                                                                         while, the urea dosage had little effect on the selectivity of TPN in the
                                                                                         aromatic nitriles, the selectivity of TPN in the aromatic nitriles remained
                                                                                         at about 90%. The suitable urea dosage for producing TPN via catalytic
                                                                                         pyrolysis of PET with urea dosage was around 1 time of PET, and the
                                                                                         mole ratio of urea to PET monomer was about 3.2.

                                                                                         3.3.2. Effect of pyrolysis temperature on the TPN production
                                                                                             The catalytic pyrolysis process from PET to TPN should undergo a
                                                                                         series of reactions, including PET decomposition, urea decomposition,
                                                                                         ammonization of pyrolysis intermediates, dehydration reactions, and
                                                                                         other cracking side reactions that were highly affected by the pyrolysis
                                                                                         temperature. Therefore, pyrolysis temperature is another key important
                                                                                         factor in TPN production. Herein, the effect of pyrolysis temperatures
                                                                                         from 500 to 700 ◦ C on the production of TPN and aromatic nitriles was
                                                                                         investigated in detail.
                                                                                             Fig. 5 indicates that pyrolysis temperature had an obvious effect on
                                                                                         the distribution of pyrolysis products. Higher pyrolysis led to more
                                                                                         intense cracking reactions and resulted in less coke and more gas
                                                                                         products. In addition, the products, such as nitriles and aromatics,
                                                                                         collected in the cold trap were also significantly affected by the pyrolysis
                                                                                         temperature. The maximum carbon yield of nitriles was obtained at
                                                                                         550 ◦ C and reached 38.7 C%. The carbon yield of nitriles started to
                                                                                         decrease with the pyrolysis temperature further increasing, which was
                                                                                         only about 17 C% at 700 ◦ C. However, for the aromatics, the higher
                                                                                         pyrolysis temperature resulted in higher carbon yields of aromatics.
                                                                                         While the pyrolysis temperature reached 700 ◦ C, the carbon yield of
                                                                                         aromatics reached about 20 C%. This could be caused by the decar­
                                                                                         boxylation and deamidation reactions of the products and intermediates
                                                                                         (such as aromatic esters, aromatic amides, and aromatic nitriles)
                                                                                         generated during the catalytic pyrolysis of urea and PET under high-

at different conversion rates.                                                           is a crucial factor for TPN production during the in situ catalytic py­

                                                                                        3.5. Possible reaction pathways from PET to TPN
3.4. Catalyst stability tests
                                                                                            To further understand the reaction mechanism of co-thermal
    The stability of commercial γ-Al2O3 catalyst during the catalytic                   decomposition of polyethylene terephthalate (PET) with urea, the DFT
pyrolysis process was studied through four reaction/regeneration cycles.                calculations were performed to explore the decomposition mechanism
In each cycle, the used γ-Al2O3 was calcined at 600 ◦ C for 4 h to remove               of terephthalic acid ethylene glycol ester dimer (1, Fig. 8). Through
the coke and regenerate it. Fig. 7 shows the carbon yields of TPN,                      comparing the three decomposition mechanisms with and without the

                                                                                    6

<!-- PDF 页 7 -->

G. Xie et al.                                                                                                          Journal of Cleaner Production 434 (2024) 140204


                                                                                     This indicates that active ammonia, such as NH3 and urea, plays a
                                                                                     prominent role in promoting the depolymerization of PET, which is
                                                                                     consistent with the results of TG kinetics calculations. Moreover, the
                                                                                     calculation results also suggest that the ammonolysis of PET was more
                                                                                     prone to form amide (1 → TS2 → 5) rather than aromatic acid ammo­
                                                                                     nium salt (1 → TS3 → 4). This also verifies that aromatic amides (such as
                                                                                     terephthalamide and benzanilide) are important intermediates in the
                                                                                     catalytic co-thermal decomposition of PET and urea for the synthesis of
                                                                                     aromatic nitriles. Based on the results, the mechanism towards the for­
                                                                                     mation of aromatic nitriles were further proposed. As shown in Fig. 8c,
                                                                                     the initial decomposition product 5 could undergo either dehydration of
                                                                                     the amide bond to nitrile (5 → TS4) or ammonolysis of another ester
                                                                                     bond to amide (5 → TS7). And the calculations revealed that ammo­
                                                                                     nolysis (151.5–164.0 kJ/mol) was more plausible than the dehydration
                                                                                     of amide (298.3–435.6 kJ/mol). Therefore, ammonolysis of the ester
                                                                                     bond in PET to form aromatic amide followed by dehydration of amide
                                                                                     to aromatic nitrile is more feasible for the co-thermal decomposition
                                                                                     mechanism of PET with urea (Fig. 8c).
     Fig. 5. Effect of pyrolysis temperature on the pyrolysis distributions.

                                                                                     4. Conclusions

                                                                                          This study has successfully synthesized aromatic nitriles, particularly
                                                                                     TPN, through in situ catalytic pyrolysis of waste PET plastic employing
                                                                                     urea as an active ammonia source and commercial γ-Al2O3 catalyst.
                                                                                     Results of TG-DTG and DAEM kinetic analysis demonstrated that posi­
                                                                                     tive interaction between the waste PET and urea led to facilitated
                                                                                     thermal decomposition. Urea dosage and pyrolysis temperature were
                                                                                     pivotal in achieving optimal TPN production. Under 550 ◦ C and urea
                                                                                     equivalent to PET, a TPN yield of 32.5% with 85% selectivity was ob­
                                                                                     tained. Furthermore, DFT calculations corroborated these findings by
                                                                                     affirming the role of active ammonia in lowering energy barriers and
                                                                                     facilitating PET depolymerization, resulting in the formation of aromatic
                                                                                     amides and subsequent dehydration to produce aromatic nitriles. The
                                                                                     study presents a novel approach method for recycling waste PET into
                                                                                     valuable aromatic nitriles through in situ catalytic pyrolysis with urea
                                                                                     and enhances the understanding of the pyrolysis process. Furthermore,
                                                                                     the pyrolysis process aligns with the principles of the circular economy
                                                                                     and the growing emphasis on reducing plastic waste, thereby demon­
Fig. 6. Effect of pyrolysis temperature on the carbon yield and selectivity of       strating a potential pathway for transforming waste into high-value
aromatic nitriles in the catalytic pyrolysis process.                                chemicals.
                                                                                          Looking forward, further research should be carried out in multiple
                                                                                     directions to ensure the successful application of this technology. Firstly,
                                                                                     it is important to explore different catalyst materials that are more stable
                                                                                     and efficient, as well as to further optimize the parameters of in situ
                                                                                     catalytic pyrolysis for the industrial production of aromatic nitriles from
                                                                                     PET. Additionally, thorough studies on the scale-up and industrializa­
                                                                                     tion potential of the proposed in situ catalytic pyrolysis process, which
                                                                                     includes techno-economic analysis, process optimization, and assess­
                                                                                     ment of environmental impact, are essential. Furthermore, it is impor­
                                                                                     tant to broaden the scope of the study to encompass a wider range of
                                                                                     waste plastics beyond PET, and to assess the potential of catalytic py­
                                                                                     rolysis with active ammonia for recycling other waste plastics into
                                                                                     valuable N-containing compounds. By addressing these research di­
                                                                                     rections, further progress can be achieved in the field of sustainable
                                                                                     plastic waste management and the production of high-value chemicals,
                                                                                     thus contributing to both environmental and economic sustainability.

Lujiang Xu a,b, Le-yao Zhang a, He Song a, Qian Dong a, Guo-hua Dong a, Xiao Kong a, Zhen Fang a,⇑

Article history:                                          Terephthalonitrile (TPN) was directly produced from polyethylene terephthalate (PET) plastic via cat-
Received 15 November 2018                                 alytic fast pyrolysis with ammonia. The optimal condition for producing TPN was over 1 g c-Al2O3-
Revised 16 April 2019                                     2 wt% catalyst at 500 °C under carrier gas (50% NH3 and 50% N2) with yield of nitriles and TPN of 58.1
Accepted 6 May 2019
                                                          and 52.3 C%, respectively. The selectivity of TPN in the nitriles was around 90%. Meanwhile, a bit of aro-
Available online 16 May 2019
                                                          matics, benzonitrile, acetonitrile were also produced as by-products with the total yields of less than 3 C
                                                          %. The catalyst deactivated slightly after 5 cycles. Possible reaction routes were proposed and it was found
Keywords:
                                                          that terephthalic acid, benzoic acid, related esters and amides were the major intermediates from PET to
Terephthalonitrile (TPN)
Polyethylene terephthalate (PET)
                                                          nitriles. Acetonitrile could be produced from acetaldehyde and its corresponding imines. In addition, 32.1
Catalytic fast pyrolysis                                  C% TPN with high purity (>95%) was obtained via freezing recrystallization. Catalytic pyrolysis with
Ammonia                                                   ammonia process was a promising technology for converting waste PET plastics to TPN. This study pro-
c-Al2O3                                                   vided a new method for producing N-containing chemicals from polyester plastics.

3.3. Effect of temperature on the terephthalonitrile (TPN) production                        temperature is a key important factor on the TPN production. Tem-
                                                                                             perature was optimized over the c-Al2O3-2% catalyst bed at the
    A number of reactions (e.g. thermal decomposition, ammonoly-                             range of 450–700 °C by fixing catalyst amount (1 g) and total gas
sis, dehydration, decarboxylation, condensation, and coupling cok-                           flow rate at 160 mL/min (50% ammonia and 50% N2). Fig. 3 shows
ing) occurred during the CFP-A of PET process. Pyrolysis                                     the effect of temperature on (a) overall yield of products, (b)
                                                                                             nitriles selectivity and (c) gas yield. In Fig. 3a, the yield of coke
                                                                                             decreased from 23.09% to 8.81%, and the yield of gases and aromat-
                                                                                             ics increased from 6.98% and 0% to 19.50% and 11.84% with tem-
                                                                                             perature increasing from 450 to 700 °C. At 450 °C, no aromatic
                                                                                             compounds were detected, while amides and oxygenates could
                                                                                             be detected, the carbon yield of them was 4.51% and 6.04%, respec-
                                                                                             tively. When temperature increased to 500 °C, no amides and oxy-
                                                                                             genates were detected, the carbon yield of nitriles reached the
                                                                                             highest yield of 58%, and decreased as temperature increased fur-
                                                                                             ther. At 700 °C, the carbon yield of nitriles was only 34.79%.
                                                                                                 Fig. 3b shows the effect of temperature on the selectivity of
                                                                                             benzonitrile, TPN, acetonitrile and other alkyl aromatic nitriles in
                                                                                             the nitriles. With temperature increasing, the selectivity of ben-
                                                                                             zonitrile, acetonitrile and other alkyl aromatic nitriles in the
                                                                                             nitriles increased, while the selectivity of TPN decreased. At
                                                                                             450 °C, the selectivity of TPN reached 94.75%. The selectivity of
                                                                                             TPN obtained at 500 °C (89.95%) was the higher as compared to
                                                                                             that at 550 °C (81.46%). At 700 °C, the selectivity of TPN was only
                                                                                             25.31%. Meanwhile, the selectivity of benzonitrile and acetonitrile
                                                                                             in the nitriles was 61.81% and 9.63%, respectively.
                                                                                                 Fig. 3c gives the carbon yield of detected gases obtained at dif-
                                                                                             ferent temperatures. With temperature increasing, the carbon
                                                                                             yield of CO, CH4 and C3H6 increased a lot. At 700 °C, the carbon
                                                                                             yield of CO reached 15.20%, which was about 4 times of that
                                                                                             obtained at 450 °C. Combined with the yield of nitriles in Fig. 3a
                                                                                             and the selectivity of TPN in Fig. 3b, the highest carbon yield of
                                                                                             TPN was 52.27% at 500 °C. Meanwhile, the selectivity of TPN was
                                                                                             89.95%. Therefore, the optimal temperature for producing TPN
                                                                                             was around 500 °C.

3.5. Effect of ammonia content in the carrier gas

                                                                                           3.6. Catalyst cycles
    Usually, nitrogen served as carrier gas in the pyrolysis process.
In the CFP-A process, ammonia is not only the carrier gas but reac-
                                                                                               The stability of catalysts was very important practical applica-
tant for producing nitriles. It is as important as PET and catalyst in
                                                                                           tions. Catalyst c-Al2O3-2% was studied by conducting five reac-
this process. However, the price of ammonia is much higher than
                                                                                           tion/regeneration cycles at the best conditions. For each cycle,
                                                                                           the spent catalyst was calcined with air (100 mL/min) at 550 °C
                                                                                           for 3 h to remove the coke formed on the surface of the catalyst.
                                                                                           Fig. 5 shows (a) overall yield (b) yield of nitriles, and (c) the selec-
                                                                                           tivity of nitriles of each catalyst recycle. After five cycles, the car-
                                                                                           bon yield of nitriles and TPN decreased from 58.11% and 52.27%
                                                                                           to 50.76% and 46.01%, respectively, slightly decreased as compared
                                                                                           with the first cycle. Meanwhile, the yield of unidentified carbon
                                                                                           increased from14.67% to 18.02%. The variation on the yield of coke,
                                                                                           gases, and aromatics was not obvious, and kept at around 16%, 10%
                                                                                           and 2%, respectively. In addition, Fig. 5c also shows that the varia-
                                                                                           tion on the selectivity of benzonitrile, TPN, acetonitrile and other
                                                                                           alkyl aromatic nitriles in the nitriles was not obvious, and kept at
                                                                                           about 7%, 90%, 2% and 1%, respectively. Based on the results, the
                                                                                           catalyst has a bit deactivation. In addition, the nitrogen adsorp-
                                                                                           tion/desorption and NH3-TPD were conducted to study the changes
                                                                                           of pore structure and total acid amounts before and after 5 cycles
                                                                                           (Table 4 and Fig. S3). In Table 4, BET surface area, pore volume
                                                                                           and pore diameter changed slightly after 5 cycles. The total acid
                                                                                           amounts decreased from 315.7 to 260.25 lmol/g. Therefore, the
                                                                                           deactivation of c-Al2O3-2% could be caused by the acid site loss.

                                                                                           3.7. Reaction pathways from PET to nitriles

                                                                                              In order to know the possible reaction pathways from PET to
                                                                                           nitriles, CFP-A of some intermediates was carried out in this study.
                                                                                           Artetxe et al. (2010) and Grause et al. (2011) reported that tereph-
                                                                                           thalic acid and benzoic acid, and their related esters were the main
                                                                                           compounds in PET thermal decomposition process. Edge et al.
                                                                                           (1996) also reported that beside the above compounds, acetalde-
                                                                                           hyde and benzaldehyde could also be formed in PET thermal
                                                                                           decomposition process. In addition, amides were the main com-
                                                                                           pounds in PET ammonolysis process. Therefore, benzoic acid,
Fig. 4. The effect of catalyst dosage on (a) overall yield, and (b) nitriles selectivity
                                                                                           methyl benzoate, benzamide, terephthalic acid, dimethyl tereph-

NH3 flow rate, 80 mL/min; PET fed at 0.5 g/batch).                                         employed as the feedstocks for producing nitriles via CFP-A process

                                                                                            stocks, the product distribution was similar to that of PET (entry 4
                                                                                            to entry 6), the main product was TPN and a little of benzonitrile
                                                                                            could also be detected. In addition, when acetaldehyde served as
                                                                                            feedstock, acetonitrile was the only kind of nitrile, benzonitrile
                                                                                            was the main product of CFP-A of benzaldehyde (entry 7 and 8).
                                                                                            It could explain the acetonitrile detected in the products of CFP-A
                                                                                            of PET.
                                                                                                Based on all the above studies, the reaction pathways from PET
                                                                                            to nitriles were proposed and summarized in Fig. 6. PET initially
                                                                                            underwent a thermal decomposition to form terephthalic acid,
                                                                                            benzoic acid, related esters, acetaldehyde and benzaldehyde. Then,
                                                                                            the aromatic acid and related esters reacted with ammonia to form
                                                                                            amides, which were converted to nitriles via dehydration. Alde-
                                                                                            hyde compounds (acetaldehyde and benzaldehyde) also reacted
                                                                                            with ammonia to form imine compounds, which were converted
                                                                                            to nitriles via dehydration. Finally, the amides and imine com-
                                                                                            pounds were converted to nitriles via catalytic dehydration reac-
                                                                                            tion. Meanwhile, some side reactions, such as decarbonylation,
                                                                                            cracking, coupling, alkylation and oligomerization, were also
                                                                                            occurred with certain amounts of coke, gases, hydrocarbons and
                                                                                            alkyl aromatic nitriles formed.

                                                                                            3.8. Separation and purification of TPN

                                                                                               The separation and purification of TPN from the pyrolysis prod-

                                                                                            other organic solvents. It could be only soluble in hot methanol
                                                                                            and benzene. In addition, benzonitrile, acetonitrile and aromatics
